And South Lyon does not have anything like the City of Brighton does. Its ordinance dealing with dangerous buildings is very antiquated and archaic. City Manager and I have looked at City of Brighton. It does have a little bit different structure, and I guess this would be the issue for discussion that I would present to council, Is in Brighton's ordinance the notice of an unsafe structure is prepared and Issued by the administration and the show cause hearing goes to the City Council and they do essentially they are the fact finder as a council body and They would take evidence and reach a conclusion as to the evidence on the The evidence presented to it and then there also is an appeal and I'm not quite sure under Brighton's ordinance how that works but it would be an appeal back to the council at the applicant Or the business owners or property owners choice and there also is a right to go to circuit court So the alternative to that which is also specifically addressed in the housing law Would be to have the administration issue the notice of dangerous building And that would be then a show cause hearing before a hearing officer who is Appointed by the city and makes those factual findings takes evidence and Issues a ruling within five days after that hearing pursuant to the statute and then the appeal would go to City Council and then there would still also be a Right to go to circuit court to appeal anything that comes out of the council as well so there Is a combination of the administrative side as well as the appeal rights administratively and To circuit court and that's generally the structure and my question for council would be Do you prefer one of those arrangements with who is the fact finder and that's the biggest question For for my purposes at this point

### Monday, April 11, 2016

[Meeting page](../../meetings/2016/2016-04-11.md) · [Full transcript](../../transcripts/2016/2016-04-11.md) · [Video](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CmsiQMSL4eE) · South Lyon City Council April 11 2016

[0:20:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CmsiQMSL4eE&t=1237s) Okay, Mr. Mayor, I can address this. At the last meeting the council approved the second reading of the mobile food Vending ordinance and asked for further information regarding the possibility of Setting a different resident and non -resident vendor fee for the six Month periods upon further research given that this involves essentially you Know engaging in business and arguably a fundamental right under the Constitution I recommend that the council set one fee for the six -month period and not Distinguish between residents and non -residents the there is a distinction I tried to address through the library fee and the community center fee there Are there are ways that residents and non -residents can be distinguished you You could do that here, But I have not been able to come up with a way where you could distinguish the Basis, uh, Come up with a justification for charging a different fee unless there were some Information where a non resident costs more to enforce against. But I don't see how that could possibly be here. So my recommendation would be one fee for all vendors.

[1:34:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CmsiQMSL4eE&t=5647s) Aye.

This has been, so there's been numerous revisions made And some research that's been provided to Oakland County Equalization, The city's assessor, several times. And the major changes, so there is a resolution That sets the policy guidelines and the application form this would all be for 2017 and the poverty exemption that's available if you meet certain criteria It's been updated for the 2017 income levels and major changes from the first Draft that council saw are we had made any any property held in trust to be ineligible based on comments from OCE they've indicated that there are Circumstances where a property held in trust can still satisfy the primary residence exemption Which would then seemingly make them available and eligible to have a poverty exemption as well If they meet the criteria so that's one of the changes we had initially included a provision for Residents 65 and older they could add one more person to the household that was removed based on concerns raised by OCE they had not seen that before and they were concerned About whether that would withstand legal challenge they've not seen it one way or The other but they were concerned about it we also removed the formula so if you recall there was a by statute you the Board of Review can grant a full or a Partial exemption the partial exemption had a formula for determining exactly what that number would be based on discussions with OCE they wanted us to remove that and provide greater flexibility to the Board of Review and Everybody is now sensitized to this issue so they also included this summary Asset and income worksheet that would help them evaluate they would they have the applicant fill it out and I think internally OCE fills that out so they Have an understanding of the person's income expenses and the assets that they They have, and then they can provide a recommendation to the Board of Review about whether a partial Is appropriate, a full, and that would provide Some flexibility based on the information received From the applicant about what the partial level, What exemption would be given on a partial basis. So there is some reasoning behind that. Also, a terminology change, the Michigan Treasury Bulletins use total household resources as opposed to household income so in dealing with OCE they gave me kind of a Crash course on that we changed that language which is not terribly a big Deal but it does help clarify what is considered to be income for purposes of The poverty exemption and also the summary worksheet is being included in The application so we would ask that the application be filled out and also that summary worksheet that OCE provided. So I want to do a little bit of an overview to give some some additional Explanation as to why that recommendation is in there there is a Little bit of history here there is no ordinance and basically the city's been Doing an ad hoc process with respect to liquor licensing so I want you to keep That in mind this ordinance is designed to comply with state law and formalize Through an ordinance the initial application process the procedures the Decision -making and then deal with some of the after approval type issues Transfers objections if you had a problem you would then have the ability To object or request a revocation of a license so the recommendation to go and Use to the earlier January 22 draft is largely based on my role as a city Attorney I am encouraging you to maintain and keep in your ordinance Tools that provide you with an ability to receive notice and make decisions on Changes or new applications for liquor licenses in your community you do always Regardless of whether you include these tools in your ordinance there is a less formal process through the MLCC which the city can provide comments or object To for example a license transfer but the trend has been for the state to try And exclude local government roles and favor the business and I don't have a Dog in the fight you as a council are going to decide how much control you Want to place on this process and what your role is going to be you've been Been provided with three different versions at least and we've gone through and dealt with some Of the issues in detail outside of this group you know I just worry that the 321 draft puts the city In a position where you're relying very heavily on the state to do work related to your local Licensing and your liquor establishments and to the extent that the state doesn't do what you like You're not going to have many tools left where they're going to take your words and your position And really do anything with it. So these are largely, there's some distinction between new applications. For example, the plan of operation in that 321 draft has been removed. And then there Are some after licenses that are issued changes, which apply to the substantial changes and the The transfers so the big change on the transfers is to limit it only to location so if there were A change in ownership you're not going to get your ordinance in three in the 321 draft would Not have any process that that liquor license holder would have to go through to transfer Ownership or sell it or anything like that if they move the location of it within your city Out of the city and I think there's less concern if somebody's bringing it into your community But you're still going to go through that process of evaluating them Like you would a new licensed applicant.
